# Escapewheel Portable Data Packet Specification, v0.2

Informational entry point.

## 1. Orientation

This document set describes a proof-of-principle portable data packet: a small semantic and logical
standard over commodity storage formats, whose packet mechanism confines no packet to a recording
modality and whose core vocabulary targets extracellular electrophysiology.
[Part 00 §1](../parts/00_architecture.md#ew-architecture-scope) defines its scope, and
[Part 00 §8](../parts/00_architecture.md#ew-numbered-part-ownership) assigns the subject and status of
every numbered part.

The design tests whether recorded data can be represented over commodity substrates:
Parquet for tables, Zarr for dense arrays, standard media containers for media, and JSON for the
manifest. **Escapewheel** is a placeholder name. The project does not require an NWB, HDMF, or PyNWB
runtime; Part 00 defines the exact scope and exclusions.

## 2. Authority and conformance

The numbered parts and the machine-readable artifacts they expressly incorporate form the current
authoritative specification candidate. Part 00 defines
[candidate and release status](../parts/00_architecture.md#ew-specification-candidate),
[authority by concern](../parts/00_architecture.md#ew-authority-by-concern), and
[internal consistency](../parts/00_architecture.md#ew-candidate-consistency).

The five conformance actors are summarized in
[Part 00 §2.4](../parts/00_architecture.md#ew-specification-actors). Packet and writer conformance are
defined in [Part 05 §10](../parts/05_validation_and_import.md#ew-conformance-classes), and verdict
composition in [Part 05 §9.5](../parts/05_validation_and_import.md#ew-verdict-composition). Reader
resolution is defined by Parts 03 and 04, and extension independence by Part 09.

This directory is the specification's tree

`parts/` and the artifacts they incorporate in `artifacts/`. `source/` holds the TOML the core
registry is assembled from, `informative/` the rendered table listing, the combined rendering and
the example documents, and `tools/` the tools that generate and check the tree, with the environment
they run in.

## 4. Packet-recorded identifiers

This table is an informative projection of the identifier-owning parts. The v0.2 specification
candidate is versioned independently of the identifiers a packet records.

| field | value | owner |
|---|---|---|
| `profile_id` | `escapewheel-portable-v0.1` | [Part 03 §1](../parts/03_portable_physical_profile.md#ew-portable-profile-scope) |
| `semantic_standard_version` | `escapewheel-semantic-v0.1` | [Part 01 §1](../parts/01_semantic_standard.md#ew-semantic-standard-scope) |
| `logical_standard_version` | `escapewheel-logical-v0.1` | [Part 02 §1](../parts/02_core_logical_schemas.md#ew-core-logical-schema-scope) |
| `registry_id` | `core` | [Part 02 §9.2](../parts/02_core_logical_schemas.md#ew-registry-self-description), core registry only |
| `registry_version` | `0.18.0` | [Part 02 §9.2](../parts/02_core_logical_schemas.md#ew-registry-self-description), core registry only |

Part 02 §9.2 defines registry self-description and compatibility. A manifest records the core
registry's `registry_id` and `registry_version` as the member name and `version` of its descriptor
in `registries` (Part 04 §8). Current development packets use core registry `0.18.0` and are
regenerated rather than treated as compatible with the earlier development shape. Each extension registry carries and advances its own `registry_version` under
the generic mechanism in Part 09; the core specification does not assign extension versions.
Part 04 §§3 and 8 define the manifest fields that record these identifiers.

## 5. Informative references

- [Apache Iceberg table specification](https://iceberg.apache.org/spec/), for the deferred
  lakehouse profile discussed in Part 00.

[Part 00 §9](../parts/00_architecture.md#ew-normative-references) defines normative-reference policy and
lists shared external authorities, among them RFC 1952, which the portable profile and the packet
manifest both invoke, and Parquet, which the portable profile and its adapter interface both invoke.
The Arrow authorities invoked by the adapter interface are in
[Part 12 §11](../parts/12_adapter_interface.md#ew-adapter-interface-normative-references). The other
references invoked only by the portable profile are in

[Part 04 §11](../parts/04_packet_catalog.md#ew-packet-catalog-normative-references).
The SQL and IEEE authorities invoked by the portable Tier 2 language are in
[Part 11 §9](../parts/11_tier_2_rule_language.md#ew-rule-language-normative-references).
The UCUM, NCBI Taxonomy, MGI, and RGD authorities invoked by logical registry declarations are in
[Part 02 §13](../parts/02_core_logical_schemas.md#ew-logical-schema-normative-references).

<!-- BEGIN 00_architecture.md -->

# Part 00 — Architecture

Normative.

<a id="ew-architecture-scope" data-spec-anchor="canonical"></a>
## 1. Scope

This part defines the Escapewheel specification framework: its authority and conformance model,
architectural classifications, numbered-part ownership and status, and publication conventions. It
also states the cross-cutting architecture of an Escapewheel packet. Detailed concept meanings,
logical declarations, physical encodings, manifest fields, and construction and validation rules
belong to the subject-owning parts listed in §8.

This specification defines a portable data packet as a small semantic and logical standard over
commodity storage formats. Its packet mechanism comprises the packet manifest (Part 04), the logical
declaration and registry model (Part 02 §§2–10), the physical profile (Parts 03 and 12), validation
and import (Part 05), extensions (Part 09), and the Tier 2 rule language (Part 11), and it confines
no packet to a recording modality. Which resources a packet carries is decided by the registries it
binds and their presence policies (Part 02 §2.5).

The **core vocabulary** comprises the concepts Part 01 defines and the declarations of the core
registry that Part 02 incorporates. It targets extracellular electrophysiology and the session it
was recorded in. The target states what the vocabulary was designed to represent and requires no
resource of any packet. Another recording modality declares the concepts and resources it needs
beyond the core vocabulary in an extension registry under Part 09 (Part 01 §2.7). They enter the
core vocabulary only through a revision of this specification under Part 09 §7. Data of another
modality that a core declaration already represents, such as a sampled quantity held as an array
asset (Part 01 §3.17), needs no extension.

The specification does not define a storage engine, a PyNWB-compatible object model, an NWB
exporter, a distributed-compute model, or ontology machinery. It has no runtime dependency on NWB, HDMF, or PyNWB, and conformance does not require a
compatibility projection into NWB.

The design objectives are that a packet be fast to query, straightforward to produce from existing
acquisition and sorting outputs, readable from more than one language, independent of HDMF as a
runtime data model, and precise enough to drive validation.

<a id="ew-specification-authority" data-spec-anchor="canonical"></a>
## 2. Specification authority and conformance

Part 00 MUST own specification-candidate identity and candidate/release terminology; conformance
targets and normative-language conventions; the normative-reference policy and shared bibliography;
actors and the terms that resolve to them; normative, informative, and mixed status; artifact
roles and authority by concern; abstraction levels and their dependency direction; packet content
classes; each numbered part's subject; and publication-state conventions.

Part 00 MUST NOT become the owner of exact keys, columns, registry counts, profile encodings,
performance measurements, implementation plans, or detailed lifecycle algorithms merely because
it summarizes the architecture. A lower-level statement that remains here during reconciliation
MUST link to its actual owner or be explicitly informative once that owner is settled.

<a id="ew-specification-candidate" data-spec-anchor="canonical"></a>
### 2.1 Specification candidate and release

The current generated v0.2 numbered parts and every artifact that a numbered part expressly
incorporates MUST constitute the authoritative working specification. An incorporated artifact
MUST participate only for the subject and extent the incorporating part states. A file outside
that set MUST NOT override it.

The **specification's tree** is the directory holding the specification README. The numbered parts
MUST lie within it, and every artifact a numbered part incorporates MUST lie in its `artifacts/`
directory, which MUST hold no other file.

Where a numbered part or an incorporated artifact gives the path of a file other than one within a
packet, and not in a link from one part to another, the path MUST be relative to the tree's root and
MUST name a file within the tree. A numbered part MAY name evidence outside the tree, such as a
conformance probe, by what it is, and MUST NOT give its path.

The working specification MUST be called a **specification candidate** until a publication decision
identifies and freezes an exact release. *Authoritative* describes what governs current development
and conformance; *candidate* describes publication state. Candidate status MUST NOT make the
working specification optional.

A **specification release** MUST be an immutable publication whose publication record identifies
its exact members and versions, and its members MUST lie within the tree. This candidate
distinguishes that state without selecting which of the tree's other files a release includes or a
publication-record format.

The specification README, `README.md`, MUST be an informational entry point. It MAY provide
navigation, candidate status, architectural summaries, build guidance, informative references,
checked status and identifier summaries, and links to owning artifacts. It MUST NOT be the sole
statement of a requirement or supply a normative preamble through its position in a rendering. The
numbered parts MUST remain intelligible without repository-specific developer documentation, which
no checker verifies. The combined-document generator MAY place the README first for orientation, but that ordering MUST NOT
supply normative force.

`informative/Escapewheel_POC_v0.2_Implementation_Spec.md` MUST be a reproducible convenience
rendering of its inputs. It MUST NOT become a separately edited owner or acquire normative force
from its placement.

<a id="ew-authority-by-concern" data-spec-anchor="canonical"></a>
### 2.2 Authority by concern

Numbered prose MUST own terms, meanings, processing semantics, actor obligations, conformance, and
relationships that a structural declaration cannot express faithfully.

A machine-readable artifact MUST acquire normative authority only when a numbered part expressly
incorporates it. It MUST own only the exact structures or assignments for which that part
incorporates it. The incorporating prose MUST continue to own the vocabulary's meaning, the
semantics of declared fields, and every applicable processing or conformance rule not fully
expressed by the artifact.

Implementations and evidence MUST NOT acquire an independent normative subject by implementing or
testing a rule.

Normativity, representation, and production MUST remain independent properties. Prose is not
normative merely because it is prose; JSON is not authoritative merely because it is structured;
generated output is not informative merely because it is generated. The declared artifact role
and subject ownership determine authority.

<a id="ew-candidate-consistency" data-spec-anchor="canonical"></a>
### 2.3 Internal consistency

Each normative fact MUST have one owner. Another artifact that presents the same fact MUST be one
of:

1. a mechanically checked projection;
2. an explicitly informative explanation that links to its owner; or
3. an explicit consistency condition between different owners' subjects.

A disagreement between owners, an unfaithful projection, or an incorporated artifact that violates
its governing prose MUST make the candidate internally inconsistent. No general prose-wins,
data-wins, generated-wins, or local-file-wins rule MAY turn that disagreement into conformance.
The inconsistent candidate remains the authoritative material to inspect and repair; a
specification release MUST NOT contain an unresolved ownership conflict.

<a id="ew-specification-actors" data-spec-anchor="canonical"></a>
### 2.4 Conformance targets and actors

Requirements in this specification apply to five actors. Every requirement binds one of them or
states a property of an artifact.

A **writer** produces a packet from sources. It stages, validates and commits. Part 05 §1 states
which of Part 05's sections bind each actor.

A **reader** opens a committed packet and returns its resources — rows, arrays, resolved references.
It is bound by what it returns.

A **checker** decides whether a document conforms to the contract governing it, and reports its
findings. The documents are a registry document under Part 02 §9.4, a physical-profile descriptor
under Part 03 §1, and a manifest under Part 04 §10.

A **validator** is the checker of a packet: it decides whether a packet conforms, and reports
findings. It checks each of those documents that the packet carries as a checker does, so a
requirement on a checker binds a validator checking one of them.

A **consumer** interprets values a reader returned and acts on them. It is bound by what it
concludes, and it is the only actor that may be a person rather than a program.

An actor is a part someone plays, not a distribution: one program is commonly all five. Naming them
separately is what lets a clause say who owes an obligation.

Three further terms resolve to those five. A **build** is one execution of a writer against one
recipe, so a requirement on a build binds the writer performing it. An **adapter** is the part of a
writer that reads one source, and an obligation on an adapter binds the writer that ships it. An
**implementation** is software performing one or more of the five, and is the right subject only
where a requirement binds every actor alike, as §3.6's ban on path-based identity does.

`importer` is another name for a writer, `client` and `binding` for a reader, and `caller` for a
consumer. A requirement using one binds the actor it names.

<a id="ew-normative-language" data-spec-anchor="canonical"></a>
### 2.5 Normative language

The key words MUST, MUST NOT, REQUIRED, SHALL, SHALL NOT, SHOULD, SHOULD NOT, RECOMMENDED, NOT
RECOMMENDED, MAY, and OPTIONAL are to be interpreted as described in BCP 14 when, and only when,
they appear in all capitals.

<a id="ew-architecture-classifications" data-spec-anchor="canonical"></a>
## 3. Architecture classifications

<a id="ew-abstraction-levels" data-spec-anchor="canonical"></a>
### 3.1 Abstraction levels

The specification MUST call semantic, logical, and physical classifications **abstraction
levels**. A lower abstraction level MUST NOT redefine a rule owned by the level above it. A
physical profile MAY map a logical resource to a storage technology and MUST NOT change that
resource's logical identity or semantic meaning. A workflow MAY describe how an actor satisfies an
owned invariant and MUST NOT restate a second version of that invariant.

<a id="ew-semantic-standard" data-spec-anchor="canonical"></a>
#### 3.1.1 Semantic standard

The **semantic standard** defines what concepts mean. A Trial is a bounded interval of experimental
time; a Unit is a sorted or curated spiking entity; an ElectricalSeries is sampled extracellular
voltage over time and channels.

The semantic standard is independent of storage format and of whether a concept is represented as
a table or an array. Part 01 specifies it.

<a id="ew-logical-standard" data-spec-anchor="canonical"></a>
#### 3.1.2 Logical standard

The **logical standard** defines how semantic concepts are represented as computable resources.
Its three logical kinds are tables, dense arrays, and opaque payloads. A view or materialization is
a realization of one of those kinds rather than another kind.

The logical standard is storage-agnostic but not shape-agnostic: trials are logically table-shaped.
Part 02 and the artifacts it incorporates specify it.

<a id="ew-physical-profile" data-spec-anchor="canonical"></a>
#### 3.1.3 Physical profile

A **physical profile** defines how logical resources are stored, and can also define the adapter
interface by which a build writing a packet under it receives what adapters read. Two are
contemplated:

| profile | tables | arrays | manifest |
|---|---|---|---|
| portable | Parquet | Zarr | JSON manifest |
| lakehouse | Iceberg over Parquet | Zarr | JSON manifest plus Iceberg catalog references |

Only the portable profile, `escapewheel-portable-v0.1`, is specified, and it defines both. Part 03
specifies how it stores a packet and is normative for a packet whose manifest declares its
`profile_id` and binds no other packet; Part 12 specifies its adapter interface. A physical profile
is the only abstraction level whose rules may depend on a storage technology, and Parts 03 and 12,
which form the physical level, are the only parts whose rules may.

<a id="ew-packet-content-classes" data-spec-anchor="canonical"></a>
### 3.2 Packet content classes

The specification MUST call content's architectural relationship to a packet its **packet content
class**. Part 02 §2.2 defines the five classes, their serialized values, and which are canonical.

Part 02 §2.2 owns which content classes a declaration may reference, require, or bind to, and
Part 09 §§6.1–6.2 own which registry it may reference and how a discriminator names a resource
without referencing it.

A packet's **canonical** resources are exactly its `core` and `extension` resources. Core is not
the whole of what is canonical: Part 09 sends a task's own facts
— which side the animal licked, whether it was rewarded — to a typed extension, and no computation
over core recovers them. Core is what every consumer can read without an extension registry. Part
02's declaration presence policies, not the content class alone, determine which core resources
every packet has.

A consumer MAY read core alone, and what it reads is complete for every core resource. It MUST NOT
conclude it has read every canonical fact the packet holds; a discriminator resolving `required`
is the case where the omission is guaranteed.

Content class MUST remain independent of abstraction level, namespace, and resource kind.
Resources of different kinds may share a content class, and resources of one kind may occupy
different content classes.

**Namespace** MUST remain the dotted-identifier ownership mechanism. Extension namespaces are
specified in Part 09. **Resource kind** MUST classify a logical resource's form independently of
its content class and physical encoding. Part 02 owns the exact serialized content-class and kind
vocabularies, declaration eligibility, presence, canonicality derivation, and lineage relation. A
packet entry names a declaration and MUST NOT repeat content class, kind, or canonicality.

<a id="ew-packet" data-spec-anchor="canonical"></a>
### 3.3 Packet

A **packet** is one concrete dataset bundle under a declared profile. It may be a local directory,
an object-store prefix, or a manifest pointing at distributed assets.

A packet contains a packet manifest, the registries it binds, and resources of the three logical
kinds (§3.1.2), each in one content class (§3.2).

A packet is primarily single-session. Multi-session packets are a permitted extension, and a
packet declares its sessions in the manifest.

<a id="ew-packet-catalog" data-spec-anchor="canonical"></a>
### 3.4 Packet manifest

The **packet manifest** is the dataset-level map. In the portable profile it is `packet.json`,
specified in Part 04.

It records which resource instances exist, the logical declarations they instantiate, their
locations, and their instance-level validation and provenance facts. The checked registry
declaration supplies logical kind, content class, canonicality, presence, and provenance policy.
The packet's profile and the declaration's kind derive a generic physical interface under Part
03; format-owned metadata supplies the representation's version and physical features.

<a id="ew-iceberg-catalog" data-spec-anchor="canonical"></a>
### 3.5 Iceberg catalog

An **Iceberg catalog** is distinct from the packet manifest. It is relevant only to the lakehouse
profile and manages only tabular resources. It does not catalog dense arrays, opaque payloads, or
semantic relationships across a packet, and therefore does not replace the packet manifest.

<a id="ew-resource-identity" data-spec-anchor="canonical"></a>
### 3.6 Resource identity

Resources and rows reference each other by stable identifier. An implementation MUST NOT use HDF5
object references, Zarr hierarchy paths, host object identity, or file layout as the primary
identity system.

<a id="ew-core-content-class" data-spec-anchor="canonical"></a>
## 4. Core content class

<a id="ew-core-content-requirements" data-spec-anchor="canonical"></a>
### 4.1 Requirements

The core content class MUST contain canonical facts in the logical schemas of Part 02. A writer
publishes a packet, core resources included, only as
[Part 05 §9.2](../parts/05_validation_and_import.md#ew-error-policy) permits. The required minimum is the closure of Part
02's declaration presence, dependency, populated-reference, and catalog rules; membership in the core class alone does not require an instance.

A fact belongs in core when common interpretation, validation, joining or analysis would break
without it (Part 09 §3). A fact a single domain owns belongs in that domain's extension, typed,
with its own vocabularies and rules — not in core, and not in a free-form column.

Core resources are normalized enough to avoid drift and no further. Part 02 §11 declares each core
resource's grain.

<a id="ew-analysis-content-class" data-spec-anchor="canonical"></a>
## 5. Analysis content class

<a id="ew-analysis-content-requirements" data-spec-anchor="canonical"></a>
### 5.1 Requirements

The analysis content class MAY contain denormalized, prejoined, binned, aligned, or otherwise
transformed products. Part 06 owns its disposability, when a view or a materialization is
justified, and its exact lineage, realization, and promotion requirements and its optional run
provenance.

## 6. Deferred NWB projection

A packet SHOULD preserve enough semantic metadata to support an NWB-like projection later. This
specification does not require PyNWB-compatible object reconstruction, NWB/HDF5 or NWB-Zarr export,
or a complete mapping onto the `NWBFile` hierarchy.

Reserved NWB names are not honoured. A projection is therefore a mapping exercise and not a
renaming.

## 7. Storage stance

### 7.1 Portable profile first

The portable profile stores tables as Parquet, dense arrays as Zarr, and each packet-owned opaque
payload as one file or one directory, under a JSON manifest. Part 03 §1 requires a packet under it
to be readable without a server.

<a id="ew-external-resource-referencing" data-spec-anchor="canonical"></a>
### 7.2 Referencing

A packet need not own every byte it describes. Externality changes ownership and what can be
inspected; it does not move format-owned metadata into the manifest. Part 04 §5 specifies how a
packet declares, records, and verifies a resource it does not own.

<a id="ew-later-physical-profiles" data-spec-anchor="canonical"></a>
### 7.3 Iceberg later

Iceberg is a later table-management profile, useful for table snapshots, schema and partition
evolution, concurrent writers, catalog integration, and reproducible release tags. An
implementation of this specification MUST NOT require it.

A physical profile — Iceberg's or any other — MUST declare a `profile_id` that a packet's manifest
names, MUST bind only the packets declaring it, MUST say how each logical resource of Part 02
is stored, and MUST say how an amendment (Part 05 §15) is realized under it: how a resource that
gains rows is stored, where superseded manifests are retained, and what a stray is. It MUST NOT require an amendment to Part 01 or Part 02; where one would be needed, the
defect is in those parts rather than in the profile. A physical profile's adapter interface binds a
build only while the build writes a packet declaring the profile, and binds an adapter only while
such a build reaches it. No clause of Part 01 or Part 02 may depend on a clause of Part 03 or
Part 12.

<a id="ew-numbered-part-ownership" data-spec-anchor="canonical"></a>
## 8. Part subjects and normative status

Part 00 MUST assign the subject and normative status of every numbered part. A local status line
MUST be a checked projection of this table and MUST NOT become a precedence source. A disagreement
MUST make the candidate inconsistent.

| part | subject | status |
|---|---|---|
| 00 | specification framework, authority, abstractions, part ownership and publication conventions | normative |
| 01 | substrate-independent meanings of scientific and provenance concepts | normative |
| 02 | logical resources, declarations, identities, references, and registry semantics | normative |
| 03 | portable physical mapping of logical resources to stored bytes | normative |
| 04 | packet manifest structure, resource entries, and dataset-to-resource bindings | normative |
| 05 | validation, import, writer and reader operations, reporting, and publication of a packet | normative |
| 06 | analysis policy, examples, and explanation | informative except §§2, 4, and 5, which are normative |
| 07 | reference-implementation design and examples | informative |
| 08 | executable packet example | informative |
| 09 | extension boundaries, namespace ownership, extension declarations, and extension independence | normative |
| 10 | performance measurements and rationale | informative |
| 11 | portable language and execution semantics for registry-declared validation rules | normative |
| 12 | exchange between a build and the adapters it reaches under the portable profile | normative |

An informative section MUST NOT be the sole statement of an obligation. A part MUST NOT state a
second version of a rule another part owns. It MAY cite the owner, or summarize the rule only when
the summary is explicitly informative.

The numbered parts MUST observe this dependency direction:

1. Part 01 defines meaning without selecting a resource shape or storage technology.
2. Part 02 and an expressly incorporated registry represent Part 01 concepts as logical resources,
   identities, relationships, and constraints without selecting a storage technology.
3. Part 11 maps Part 02's declared resources and types into portable rule evaluation without
   redefining logical declarations or Part 05's validation and reporting workflow.
4. Part 03 maps logical resources to bytes under a profile without changing their logical identity
   or semantic meaning.
5. Part 04 binds concrete packet resources to the semantic, logical, registry, and profile
   identifiers that govern them without redefining those contracts.
6. Part 05 specifies how writers, readers, and validators construct and judge instances under the
   upstream contracts rather than becoming a second owner of those contracts.
7. Part 12 specifies what a build and the adapters it reaches exchange under a profile, within
   Part 05's import pipeline, without changing Part 02's logical resources and identities or how
   Part 03 stores them; Part 05 cites it for what a build supplies an adapter and what a delivery
   states.
8. Part 09 permits namespaced extension declarations under the same applicable semantic, logical,
   physical, manifest, and conformance boundaries without altering core.

A writer MUST interpret adapter declarations and output under the owning numbered parts and
incorporated declarations. An adapter's participation in ingestion MUST NOT let it redefine the
packet contract. Authority follows express incorporation and subject ownership, not the direction
of a software call or generation step.

A future release manifest MAY record numbered-part status, but that value MUST be a checked
projection of this section. The manifest MUST NOT become a second owner of numbered-part status.

<a id="ew-normative-references" data-spec-anchor="canonical"></a>
## 9. Normative-reference policy and shared references

Part 00 MUST own the specification-wide policy for external normative references. An external
authority that normative prose in exactly one numbered part invokes directly MUST have its
bibliographic entry in a normative-reference section of that part. An external authority that
normative prose in more than one numbered part invokes directly, or that the Part 00 framework
itself invokes, MUST have one shared bibliographic entry in Part 00.

Normative prose invokes an external authority **directly** when it identifies that authority as
the basis for one or more requirements. A citation to another numbered part's Escapewheel contract
is not a direct invocation of the external authorities on which that contract relies.

Each direct external normative authority MUST have exactly one bibliographic entry in the numbered
parts. Each part whose normative prose invokes an external authority directly MUST identify the
applicable entry from the prose that establishes the authority's use and MUST state the extent to
which it applies. That invocation MAY govern a defined group of requirements and need not be
repeated in each requirement. A bibliographic entry identifies the external document and edition;
it MUST NOT become the owner of the Escapewheel meaning, processing rule, actor obligation, or
conformance behavior that invokes it.

An informative mention of a technology MUST NOT make its specification normative. Moving a
bibliographic entry MUST NOT change the referenced behavior, and a reference used by several
parts through direct invocation MUST NOT be copied into several normative-reference sections. The
informational specification README MUST NOT contain a normative-reference section or become an
independent bibliographic owner.

An **exact** bibliographic entry MUST identify the edition used by the candidate. An
**unversioned** entry MUST be identified as such and follows the linked external specification
during candidate development. A **living-registry** entry identifies a registry that assigns names
or identifiers as they are registered, by the registry's name and URI, and gives `living registry`
as its candidate edition. A numbered part MAY give an authority a living-registry entry only where
the authority is such a registry and no validator verdict depends on its content. A requirement
invoking a living registry refers to the registry as it stood when the bound actor acted; for a
spelling requirement, when the writer wrote the value, so a later registration or renaming leaves
the value conforming.

A specification release MUST identify the exact external edition on which every conformance
requirement depends, and MUST identify each living registry by its entry's name and URI in place of
an edition.

A normative reference's own dependencies MUST apply through that reference and need not be copied
into an Escapewheel reference section unless this specification relies on them directly. Where
required behavior has no sufficient language-neutral authority, a numbered part MUST NOT promote
an implementation or library's observed behavior into a normative reference. The missing authority
MUST remain a candidate defect and MUST prevent release until the subject-owning part defines the
behavior or cites an adequate specification.

The application column states the extent to which the specification invokes each shared authority:

| reference | candidate edition | application |
|---|---|---|
| [BCP 14](https://www.rfc-editor.org/info/bcp14), comprising RFC 2119 and RFC 8174 | exact RFC editions | normative keywords |
| [RFC 8259](https://www.rfc-editor.org/info/rfc8259) | exact | JSON |
| [RFC 3986](https://www.rfc-editor.org/info/rfc3986) | exact | URI syntax |
| [JSON Schema Core](https://json-schema.org/draft/2020-12/json-schema-core) and [Validation](https://json-schema.org/draft/2020-12/json-schema-validation) | Draft 2020-12 | incorporated JSON Schemas |
| [RFC 1952](https://www.rfc-editor.org/info/rfc1952) | exact | gzip member format of a bound registry (Part 03 §3.3) and CRC-32 (Part 04 §4.3) |
| [Apache Parquet format](https://github.com/apache/parquet-format), including [logical types](https://github.com/apache/parquet-format/blob/master/LogicalTypes.md) | unversioned | Parquet files and logical types of stored tables (Part 03 §5) and of `files-v0.1` deliveries (Part 12 §3) |

The missing-authority release block applies currently to any required Zarr codec configuration or
binary behavior not determined by the authorities in
[Part 03 §12](../parts/03_portable_physical_profile.md#ew-portable-profile-normative-references).

<!-- END 00_architecture.md -->

<!-- BEGIN 01_semantic_standard.md -->

# Part 01 — Semantic Standard

Normative.

<a id="ew-semantic-standard-scope" data-spec-anchor="canonical"></a>
## 1. Scope

This part defines what the concepts in a packet mean. It does not decide how they are stored.

The semantic standard defined by this part is identified by `escapewheel-semantic-v0.1`.

Every semantic concept MUST be representable in the logical standard (Part 02). The semantic standard itself is independent of Parquet, Zarr, Iceberg, or any other substrate.

## 2. Global semantic rules

<a id="ew-stable-identity" data-spec-anchor="canonical"></a>
### 2.1 Stable identity

Any object referenced by another object MUST have a stable identifier. A reference names its target by the identifier's value, never by a row's position in a file, so reordering a table's rows changes no reference. Part 02 §5.3 fixes each identifier column's type, and Part 04 §4.1 fixes the form of a resource ID.

Each identifier declares its **identity**:

| identity | definition |
|---|---|
| `carried` | the source's own value, reproduced unchanged, whatever the source encoded in it |
| `assigned` | chosen by the writer when the packet was built |

A writer MUST NOT encode meaning in the value of an identifier it assigns. No checker verifies this. An assigned identifier is paired with the name it was assigned from, so that a process outside the packet can reference the object without knowing the number; Part 02 §8.2 declares the pairing.

<a id="ew-time-basis" data-spec-anchor="canonical"></a>
### 2.2 Time basis

Every event, interval, spike time, dense time axis, and media frame timing record MUST declare a time basis: an integer tick and a `clock_id`.

A session's **time origin** is a tick of its reference clock: the earliest observation-interval start among the session's clocks, each mapped onto the reference clock (§3.9). A writer declaring an origin MUST declare that one. A writer MUST NOT declare an origin for a session with no observation interval, and no checker verifies this. A writer unable to map one of the session's observation starts onto the reference clock MUST NOT declare an origin: an origin it could not compute shifts every second a consumer derives. Part 05 §5.3 states how a validator checks a declared origin.

Floating-point seconds MUST NOT be the canonical representation. Seconds MAY be derived: a tick's seconds are its distance in ticks from the origin on the reference clock, divided by that clock's rational rate, and are negative for a tick before the origin. A consumer lacking an origin MUST refuse to derive seconds rather than assume one.

A tick is a coordinate, not a label and not a quantity. A writer MUST NOT re-base a source's sample index: for a sample clock, tick 0 is sample 0.

### 2.3 Observation

A session's extent is stated as **wall-clock time**, not as a tick range. A hull over every clock's ticks would need those ticks converted to one clock, and alignment may be anchor-based — outside the first and last anchor the mapping is undefined, which is exactly where a hull reaches. A session therefore declares when it started and MAY declare when it ended, both as instants.

A clock is observing during declared intervals, in that clock's own ticks. Anything timed by a clock inherits that clock's window and MAY refine it, but MUST NOT assert a window derived from its own timestamps. A clock's own interval is not derived from what was timed on it either: it is the extent of the acquisition, so for a sample clock whose origin is sample 0 of a recording it runs from tick 0 to the last sample's index, one less than the recording's length.

An interval is **closed**: `start_tick` and `stop_tick` both name observed ticks, so an interval covers `stop - start + 1` ticks and a one-tick interval has `start == stop`. A consumer MUST NOT treat a `stop_tick` as excluded. No checker verifies this.

### 2.4 Provenance

An imported core resource SHOULD be traceable to source data or source activities, and a derived analysis resource MUST declare lineage to the core resources it derives from.

Provenance is a source-file record, a processing-run record, and a link between them. A packet carrying no provenance is conforming; where provenance is present it is validated.

<a id="ew-units-of-measure" data-spec-anchor="canonical"></a>
### 2.5 Units of measure

A measured quantity SHOULD declare its unit. A stored integer requiring conversion to physical units MUST declare the conversion.

A quantity's unit is declared, not left to the quantity's name.

### 2.6 Currency

Where several derived results of the same kind and scope coexist, a consumer MUST NOT silently choose between them: it refuses, or reports the ambiguity. A preference is declared, keyed on the producing run, and a consumer selecting derived data for an activity SHOULD use the preferred run where one is declared for the matching scope — a declared preference a consumer ignores is a preference for nobody.

A run declares the scope it is about. A run declaring none is **undeterminable**, which a consumer MUST report separately from ambiguous, and the state is contagious: where more than one run exists for an `activity_type` and **any** of them declares no scope, a consumer MUST NOT conclude the group is unambiguous. The unscoped run may or may not be about the same thing as the others, and nothing in the packet says which.

Where a run supersedes another, the chain MUST be well-founded: a run MUST NOT supersede itself, directly or transitively.

### 2.7 Extension

Lab-specific concepts MAY be added through namespaced extensions (Part 09). An extension MUST NOT alter the meaning of a core concept without a new semantic version.

## 3. Concepts

### 3.1 Dataset

A collection of packets sharing a semantic and logical standard. It is a organizational concept and has no required representation.

### 3.2 Packet

One concrete bundle of resources under a declared physical profile. A packet MUST declare the sessions it contains.

<a id="ew-session-concept" data-spec-anchor="canonical"></a>
### 3.3 Session

One continuous recording engagement with one subject.

- MUST have an identifier and a subject.
- MUST declare the clock its times are reported on where it declares more than one clock, and
  SHOULD declare it where it declares one.
- MUST declare its time origin where seconds are to be derived.

### 3.4 Subject

The animal or preparation recorded from.

- MUST have an identifier.
- SHOULD declare species, sex, and date of birth.

<a id="ew-device-concept" data-spec-anchor="canonical"></a>
### 3.5 Device

A physical instrument used during acquisition.

- MUST have an identifier.
- SHOULD declare manufacturer, model, and serial number where its source states them.
- SHOULD declare the **hardware revision** and **firmware version** it was running.
- SHOULD name the device it was **attached to**, where a source states it.
- SHOULD declare **which port** of that device it occupied, where a source states it.

A serial number identifies the object; its firmware determines what the object does. Two sessions can record the same make, model and serial and need interpreting differently, so a version is not an identifier's decoration but a separate fact.

A consumer MAY walk from a probe to the hardware that acquired it, and MAY group probes by any element of the chain they share — which is the question the chain exists to answer. It MUST NOT infer attachment from a device's name: a name is unconstrained by this standard, and the reference is the statement.

Attachment names a device, and which place on that device a child occupied is a second fact. A device SHOULD declare the port, dock or slot of its parent it attached to, where a source states one. Where it declares none the coordinate is **unrecorded**, and a consumer MUST report it as such rather than parse it out of a name. Two devices sharing a parent and a port are not one device, and nothing declares that they are: the four bases of a quad-base probe occupy a single dock, so a port says where something was plugged in and not what it was. A consumer MUST NOT conclude from a shared parent and port that two devices are one. No checker verifies this.

A device is the **physical object**. Where a source presents several recording entities from one object — the bases of a quad-base probe — each is a Probe and all name the same Device; a writer MUST NOT record a second device for one instrument. Attachment is stated, never inferred from a name, and the chain MUST terminate: a device MUST NOT be its own ancestor.

A writer records both versions **verbatim** as the source stated them, and MUST NOT normalise, pad, or parse them into components. Neither is ordered: a consumer MAY compare two version strings for equality and MUST NOT compare them for precedence, because different manufacturers' schemes share none.

Where an element of an acquisition chain reports its own versions, it is its own device. A value the source gives as unknown is recorded as the source gave it, which is distinct from a source that said nothing.

This standard names no kinds of device, so a writer MUST record every instrument its source declares — a camera on the same terms as a headstage — and identity is the serial the source states. A device does not record where an instrument was pointed: this standard defines no fact for a camera's target, and it stays in `metadata` until something has to read it.

### 3.6 Probe

A device that carries electrodes.

- MUST belong to a session's device inventory.
- MAY declare manufacturer, part number, and geometry.

### 3.7 Electrode

One recording contact.

- MUST belong to one probe.
- MAY have a position in one or more coordinate frames.

<a id="ew-coordinate-concept" data-spec-anchor="canonical"></a>
### 3.8 Coordinate

A position of an electrode or unit in a declared frame.

- MUST name a frame the packet declares (§3.24).
- MUST carry each of its values on an axis that frame declares, and MAY carry values on fewer axes
  than the frame declares where its source states none on the rest.
- MUST declare the **basis** of its claim:

| basis | meaning |
|---|---|
| `planned` | where the probe was meant to go; intent, and possibly missed |
| `measured` | where it was, established after the fact by observation |
| `nominal` | where it is by construction; nothing aimed at it and nothing observed it |

The vocabulary is open. A consumer that does not recognise a basis MUST NOT treat the coordinate as `measured`.

A position is as many numbers as its frame has axes rather than a fixed three, each a value Part 02 §12.7 represents. Each value's unit is the axis's, and its meaning is what the axis says it holds.

A coordinate MAY carry an atlas annotation: the structure the position falls in, as an identifier and a label. An electrode is not in a region; a position is, so the annotation belongs to the coordinate rather than to the contact.

Where the identifier is present the label SHOULD be too, so a consumer without the atlas can still read the annotation. Part 02 §12.8 limits what a registry document declares for it.

A validator MUST NOT treat an absent annotation as a finding. Where a position falls outside any declared structure, or no registration covered it, the packet records a fact about the session.

A coordinate SHOULD name the processing run that produced it where the writer knows it. That is provenance — which run produced the coordinate — and not a choice between coordinates.

<a id="ew-clock-concept" data-spec-anchor="canonical"></a>
### 3.9 Clock

A monotonic counter with a declared rate and origin.

- MUST declare a tick rate, as a rational where the rate is not an integer.
- MAY declare a parent clock, and if so MUST be alignable to it.
- MUST declare either a linear transform or anchor points, never both.
- MAY bind its **tick 0 to a wall-clock instant**, which is the only route from a
  packet's ticks to real time; absent it, ticks relate to each other and to nothing else.
- A counter that resets mid-session is two clocks, one per epoch, each with its own
  identifier, origin and wall-clock binding. A packet MUST NOT represent a reset as a
  discontinuity within one clock: an anchor sequence requires both sides to increase, so there
  is no way to express it and no way to read it.

Where a clock binds its tick 0 to a wall-clock instant, the wall-clock time of a tick on that clock
is that instant plus the tick divided by the clock's declared tick rate, in seconds.

A clock's tick-zero binding and a session's start time are **independent and MUST NOT be
conflated**. One is a property of a counter, the other a fact about when work began.
Where a wall-clock binding is derived rather than declared, the tick it is anchored on MUST lie
in the same epoch as the instant it is paired with — a binding computed across a reset
describes neither clock.

A tick's zero is where the clock's declared origin says it is — for a sample clock whose origin is
sample 0 of a recording, tick 0 **is** that sample. A writer MUST NOT re-base a source's sample
index and a consumer MUST NOT assume any base beyond what the origin declares; that
prohibition is what makes a tick exact.

A clock's **median anchor interval** is the median interval between its consecutive anchors, in ticks on that clock. Between two anchors separated by at most two median anchor intervals, the mapping onto the parent clock is linear interpolation on the bracketing pair.

Beyond the terminal anchors a consumer MUST compute the mapping as `parent(t) = parent(a) + rate * (t - a)`, where `a` is the nearest terminal anchor and `rate` is fitted over the clock's full anchor sequence. A consumer MUST NOT extend the terminal segment's slope, and MUST NOT use the intercept of a fit over the whole anchor sequence. No checker verifies any of the three.

Alignment beyond the terminal anchors is defined only while the estimator's error stays within tolerance, 3 ms at p95 by default. A profile or a recipe MAY state a different tolerance.

A writer reaching past one median anchor interval MUST measure its error; no checker verifies that it did. Part 02 §12.6 owns what the writer records of the tolerance and the measurement. Where a tick is mapped through a chain of parent clocks, how far each clock's mapping reaches is counted in that clock's own median anchor interval.

A consumer MUST **report** ticks it cannot map rather than omitting them, so a conversion returns a result for every tick it is given, marked undefined where the mapping is. A tick that vanishes in conversion is indistinguishable from one that was never there. It MUST record how many ticks it dropped for falling outside the bound **even when that count is zero**: silence is what lets a reader and a writer disagree about which values exist.

### 3.10 Interval

A bounded span of experimental time within a session.

- MUST belong to one session.
- MUST have start and stop ticks on a declared clock, closed per §2.3.
- MUST declare its **kind**: the resource that specialises it.

A trial is one kind of interval. Blocks, epochs, and stimulation periods are others. What an interval *means* — condition, stimulus, choice, outcome, stimulation parameters — is declared by the specialising resource and MUST NOT be added to the core interval.

### 3.11 Event

An instantaneous or near-instantaneous timestamped observation: a tick, and the stream it was
observed on.

- MUST belong to one session and to one event stream.
- MUST have a tick, on the clock its stream declares.
- MAY carry a structured payload.

An event carries no type and no clock of its own: both are properties of the stream, which emits
one kind of event and declares one clock. An event does not reference an interval. Examples:
stimulus onset, reward delivery, lick, threshold crossing, TTL edge,
synchronization pulse.

<a id="ew-event-stream-concept" data-spec-anchor="canonical"></a>
### 3.12 Event stream

The context a class of events was observed in: what produced them, on what clock, what kind of
event they are, and where anything further about them lives.

- MUST declare an event type, from an open vocabulary, and the clock its events are timed on.
- MAY name a device, a processing run, and a declaration specialising its events.

A stream emits **one kind** of event, so a source emitting several on one channel is written as
several streams. What a stream's events carry beyond a tick is that named declaration's to declare, not
core's: how many marks an event has and of what type is a property of what produced it.

An event stream that names no processing run states only that the packet names no run that produced
its events. A consumer MUST NOT conclude from an event stream naming no processing run that its
events came off a device as recorded. A consumer that reports a stream's producing run MUST report,
for such a stream, that the packet names no producing run. A consumer that reports no stream's
producing run owes only the first of these. No checker verifies either.

<a id="ew-unit-concept" data-spec-anchor="canonical"></a>
### 3.13 Unit

A sorted or curated spiking entity.

- MUST belong to one sorting run and one curation version.
- MAY sit on several electrodes, and MAY have a position in one or more frames.

A unit is not a neuron. It is what a sorting produced.

### 3.13.1 A rate needs a denominator

A firing rate is a count over a duration, so the duration MUST be knowable. Where a unit declares observation intervals, the rate is its spike count over the total of those intervals; where it declares none, it is the count over the recording's extent. A packet SHOULD state which denominator it used, because the two differ for any unit that drifted out and back.

### 3.14 Spike event

One detected action potential of one unit, at one tick on one clock. Spike events are the canonical spiking representation.

### 3.15 Quality label

An assessment of a unit by one scorer. A unit has many, not one.

- MUST name the source that assigned it.
- MAY carry the source's own label verbatim, which is open.
- MAY carry a normalized label from a closed vocabulary.

### 3.16 ElectricalSeries

Sampled extracellular voltage over time and channels, represented as a dense array asset with a declared time axis and channel mapping.

<a id="ew-array-asset-concept" data-spec-anchor="canonical"></a>
### 3.17 Array asset

A dense array with declared axes. An axis declares its coordinates; a regularly sampled axis
declares them as an origin, a step, and a length rather than as stored values.

An array asset holds **any** sampled quantity, whatever instrument produced it. This standard names the concepts it defines — §3.16 is the one it defines for extracellular voltage — and it does not enumerate instruments. A packet names its own arrays, and an array names the device that made it (§3.5); a rig fitted with an instrument this standard has never heard of therefore needs no extension, no new concept, and no revision.

`semantic_type` is open. A value this standard does not define is a label and carries no contract, which is the honest state of affairs for a quantity whose meaning lives in the device rather than in the format.

It is also **optional**, and absent is the ordinary case: a writer MUST NOT state it where the asset's other declared facts already say what it holds. A label that carries no contract and that every asset must state is one a writer invents a value for. What remains is what the facts cannot express: which statistic of a waveform an array holds, or what a video shows, neither of which any other fact carries.

Which **band** of a signal an array holds is a separate question, answered by a fact of its own: `wideband`, `ap` and `lfp` are reserved for the corresponding signal bands, the vocabulary is open, and an array not split into bands declares none. A band value is a conventional shorthand for a filter the packet does not record, so a consumer MUST NOT read it as a statement of the filter's corners, and MUST NOT infer a band from an asset's `semantic_type`, its device or its sampling rate — a signal can be decimated without being filtered and filtered without being decimated.

An array asset SHOULD name the device that produced it, where the writer knows which one did. A
consumer MAY then resolve any array to its instrument without knowing what kind of array it is,
and MUST NOT infer an instrument from an asset's `semantic_type` or name: those are labels, and the
device is a reference. Parts 02 and 06 own logical declaration and resource-level provenance;
Parts 03 and 04 own physical encoding, location, and packet binding.

Where a packet records the processing run that produced an array and the writer knows which run
that is, the writer MUST name that run on the array asset, and it names no run for an array that no
run the packet records produced. No checker verifies this.

An array asset that names no producing run states only that the packet names no run that produced
the array. A consumer MUST NOT conclude from an array asset naming no producing run that the array
was acquired. A consumer that reports an array's producing run MUST report, for such an array, that
the packet names no producing run. A consumer that reports no array's producing run owes only the
first of these. No checker verifies either.

<a id="ew-media-asset-concept" data-spec-anchor="canonical"></a>
### 3.18 Media asset

An image or video, and the timing of its frames.

- MUST have a stable media asset ID.
- SHOULD record each frame's timing where synchronization matters.

A frame is indexed by **presentation order**: index *n* is the frame the asset presents nth, counting from 0. A writer MUST NOT number frames in the order the container stores them, which differs wherever a codec reorders — a stream with bidirectional frames displaces one by several positions, and the resulting misattribution of time is monotonic and therefore invisible.

The index counts the frames the asset **holds**, not the exposures the capture produced. A frame lost before or during writing is not recorded and reserves no index; what went missing is recorded in the frames' timing, not as a gap in the numbering. A consumer MUST NOT read a frame's index as a count of exposures, as a camera's own frame counter, or as an offset into anything but this asset. No checker verifies this.

A frame's time is a tick on a declared clock, like every other time in a packet (§2.1). A container's own **presentation timestamp** is a different quantity and is carried separately: an integer in the container's own time base, verbatim. A writer MUST NOT rescale it to seconds, to a clock's ticks, or to a frame number, and MUST leave it absent where it did not read the container. It is not a time on any clock the packet declares, so a consumer MUST NOT compare it between assets, use it to measure elapsed time, or use it to align a frame to anything else in the packet; what it locates is the frame inside the file. No checker verifies what a consumer does with it.

A consumer MAY pass a presentation timestamp verbatim to a container library to seek the frame it was recorded for, which is the exact route and assumes nothing about ordering.

The asset declares the **rate** those timestamps count at, as an exact rational — a container time base of 1/16000 s is 16000 over 1, in the shape a clock's tick rate already uses. A writer that carries presentation timestamps MUST declare it and MUST NOT declare half of it. A consumer MAY then convert a timestamp to seconds of the asset's own timeline without opening the media, which is the case a referenced asset makes ordinary: the file is the part a packet is least likely to have. A writer that carries no presentation timestamps for an asset MUST NOT declare a rate for it, and no checker verifies this.

A writer MUST name the device that recorded a media asset where its source states one, and MUST
NOT infer it from a file name. No checker verifies either. Nothing else separates two such assets: a behaviour video and a movie from a
head-mounted microscope may both be written by a camera, and `semantic_type` and the asset's name
are labels rather than references. Where the source states no device, none is recorded, and a
consumer MUST report the instrument as unrecorded rather than read one out of the asset's name.
Parts 02–04 own its declaration, physical encoding, location, and packet binding.

### 3.19 Source file

An input the packet was built from.

### 3.20 Processing run

An activity that produced resources, declaring what it was, what it ran on, and the scope it was about.

### 3.21 Sorting run

A processing run that produced units from a recording, declaring the clock its ticks are on.

### 3.22 Curation version

A named state of a sorting's unit set, optionally derived from a parent curation.

<a id="ew-calibration-concept" data-spec-anchor="canonical"></a>
### 3.23 Calibration

What someone measured about an instrument so that its numbers can be interpreted: how many microlitres a valve delivers when held open for 30 ms, how much light a fibre emits at a given drive, where a manipulator's own coordinates sit in the rig, where a camera is mounted.

- MUST have an identifier, a name, and a declared type from an open vocabulary.
- MUST name the session it was in force for.
- SHOULD declare when it was measured, where the source states one.
- MAY name the devices it covers, carry the points of its curve, or name the array holding it.

A calibration is a standing property of an instrument rather than an activity of this packet, so a session reports it as **in force** rather than as having happened, and it is not a Processing run.

One calibration may cover many devices, so which devices it covers is recorded per device and not as a property of the calibration.

A source states a calibration in at least four ways: as a curve, as an array, as a date and nothing else, and as an assertion that none was made. A calibration with no recorded content is still a calibration, and a consumer MUST read such a calibration as **unrecorded** rather than as absent. A source stating that no calibration was performed records none: the absence of a calibration is not a calibration.

<a id="ew-coordinate-frame-concept" data-spec-anchor="canonical"></a>
### 3.24 Coordinate frame

The space a coordinate is a position in, together with its axes.

- MUST have a name, which is the session's own name for that space.
- MUST declare each axis it has, each carrying the source's own name for what that axis holds;
  Part 02 §12.7 represents them.
- MAY name the published atlas the space is, and the version of that atlas.
- MAY declare which way an axis's values increase, and MUST leave that undeclared where the source does not state it.

A frame has as many axes as it has: two for a shank, five for a rendering space.

A writer MUST declare each axis's unit, which it knows because it wrote the values. No checker verifies this.

An axis's name is the source's word for the field its values came from, carried verbatim — `ccf_ml` where the source's electrodes table has a column of that name. It records where the value came from and not what the quantity is conventionally taken to be, so a consumer needing to know which way an axis runs, where no direction is declared, MUST report the coordinate unusable for that purpose rather than assume a convention.

Which published atlas a frame is, is a fact the frame declares rather than a spelling of its name: two packets both naming a frame `ccf` may be registered against CCFv3 and against a successor whose structure ids differ, and comparing names cannot tell them apart. A writer MUST NOT fill the atlas or its version from the name.

A frame belongs to one session, because a name two sessions reuse may name different spaces — the reason `core.devices` is session-scoped.

<!-- END 01_semantic_standard.md -->

<!-- BEGIN 02_core_logical_schemas.md -->

# Part 02 — Core Logical Schemas

Normative.

<a id="ew-core-logical-schema-scope" data-spec-anchor="canonical"></a>
## 1. Scope

This part defines how a logical resource is declared, what each kind of declaration contains, and
which core and analysis resources the incorporated registry comprises.

The logical standard defined by this part is identified by `escapewheel-logical-v0.1`.

The column-level definitions themselves are not written here. They are data (§9), carried in a registry document that a packet embeds and that generates the checks of Part 05. This part is the rules the registry obeys; the registry is the schema.

This part expressly incorporates the following artifacts:

| artifact | normative extent |
|---|---|
| `artifacts/registry.schema.json` | exact local JSON structure and conditional shape constraints |
| `artifacts/registry_contract.schema.json` | exact structure of a registry-contract descriptor |
| `artifacts/registry_contracts/escapewheel-registry-contract-v0.10.json` | exact contract selectors, phase order, invariant IDs, applicability, and owning anchors |
| `artifacts/logical_schemas.json` | exact current core and analysis resource declarations, including table columns, keys, references, vocabularies, and rules |

The generated core registry is what a packet embeds. `informative/tables.md` is a generated,
informative listing of the same declarations. This part owns the declaration model, conformance
algorithms, and meanings. A disagreement among the part and its incorporated artifacts makes the
specification candidate internally inconsistent.

<a id="ew-table-resource-requirements" data-spec-anchor="canonical"></a>
## 2. Logical resource declarations

Each member name of the root `resources` object is a declaration ID. A declaration ID MUST match
`^[a-z][a-z0-9_]*\.[a-z][a-z0-9_]*$`. The core registry is the exact declaration authority for
the `core` and `analysis` namespaces. An extension registry is the sole declaration authority for
the namespace equal to its `extension_id`.

A generated table-only implementation view MAY select table-kind declarations, but it MUST be
derived from `resources` and MUST NOT be serialized as a second authority.

<a id="ew-resource-common-fields" data-spec-anchor="canonical"></a>
### 2.1 Common fields

Every resource declaration contains exactly these common fields, plus the kind-specific field §2.3
permits:

- `content_class`, governed by §2.2;
- `kind`, one of `table`, `dense_array`, and `opaque`;
- `instances`, a closed object governed by §2.4;
- `requires`, an array of distinct declaration IDs, empty where there is no explicit dependency;
  and
- `lineage`, one of `forbidden`, `optional`, and `required`.

<a id="ew-resource-content-class" data-spec-anchor="canonical"></a>
### 2.2 Content class and canonicality

The `content_class` vocabulary and its consequences are:

| value | meaning | canonical | required by class |
|---|---|:---:|:---:|
| `core` | normalized facts of the core vocabulary | yes | no |
| `extension` | typed facts owned by a domain | yes | no |
| `analysis` | products reproducible from canonical resources | no | no |
| `compatibility_projection` | a projection into another representation | no | no |
| `documentation` | sidecars, which a packet lists and asserts no semantic or conformance fact through, and the tables that list them | no | no |

Canonicality is derived from this table and MUST NOT be independently declared. Promoting a
resource into the canonical set changes its declaration's content class through a specification
or extension-registry change; an implementation MUST NOT promote it with a Boolean.

A **sidecar** is an opaque resource of class `documentation`: one that a packet lists and through
which it asserts no semantic or conformance fact. The class also covers the tables that list
sidecars (§12.10). A sidecar MAY be read by a person or by a program. This specification defines no
behavior of a reader, a writer or a validator that interprets a sidecar's contents.

Listing a resource as a sidecar records no dependence on it: the rule below forbids a canonical
declaration a reference, a `requires` entry or a catalog binding that targets a documentation
declaration, and §2.7 permits no lineage from one. A registry's author who needs a table to
reference a payload, or a rule or a reader to interpret one, therefore declares it as a canonical
resource. A machine-interpreted control artifact does not acquire documentation semantics merely
because its serialization is a document. A payload that a result in the packet was computed from is
recorded by the provenance tables ([Part 04 §7](../parts/04_packet_catalog.md#ew-resource-lineage)), and the
same payload MAY also be listed as a sidecar.

The core registry MUST NOT declare `extension`. An extension registry MAY declare `extension`,
`analysis`, `compatibility_projection`, or `documentation` in its own namespace and MUST NOT
declare `core`. Every `analysis` and `compatibility_projection` declaration requires lineage.
Every `documentation` declaration forbids lineage. These three
classes are optional under §2.5.

A registry document MUST NOT declare a `core` or `extension` resource that has a table reference,
a `requires` entry or a catalog binding targeting an `analysis`, `compatibility_projection` or
`documentation` declaration. A non-canonical resource holds no fact a canonical one may depend on,
and a writer may delete any analysis resource
([Part 06 §2](../parts/06_analysis_layer.md#ew-analysis-rules)), which would leave a canonical
resource depending on one without its target. §2.7 states the same relation for lineage. A
discriminator names a resource without depending on it
([Part 09 §6.2](../parts/09_metadata_and_extensions.md#ew-core-extension-discrimination)), so this rule
does not reach one. Which registry a declaration may target is
[Part 09 §6.1](../parts/09_metadata_and_extensions.md#ew-core-extension-reference-direction)'s rule.

<a id="ew-resource-kind" data-spec-anchor="canonical"></a>
### 2.3 Kind

The logical kind states which generic payload structure, if any, Escapewheel interprets:

| kind | generic payload contract | kind-specific declaration |
|---|---|---|
| `table` | rows and declared columns | required `table` object |
| `dense_array` | an n-dimensional dense array with declared logical axes | required closed `array` object containing `axes_resource` |
| `opaque` | a payload with no generic Escapewheel logical model | none |

A table declaration MUST contain `table`, MUST NOT contain `array`, and MUST use singleton
instances. Its `table` object contains an ordered column list (§3), a primary key or `null`,
references (§7), Tier 2 rules (§10.2), and amendment groups (§2.8).

A dense-array declaration MUST contain `array`, MUST NOT contain `table`, and MUST use catalog
instances. Its `axes_resource` MUST name a visible singleton table declaration. The axes table MUST
have exactly one binding reference to the array declaration's catalog table: the reference targets
the complete catalog primary key, its local columns lead the axes table's primary key, and that
primary key adds exactly one required structural-index column. Presence of a dense-array instance
makes its axes-table singleton necessary. Part 03 follows this declaration and MUST NOT hardcode an
axes resource.

An opaque declaration contains neither kind-specific object and may use any instance mode. Opaque
qualifies only the generic payload model. Escapewheel still identifies, locates, integrity-checks,
and records provenance for the resource; related declarations and tables may describe its
scientific meaning. Generic code is not thereby required to parse, decode, or render its payload.

Stored materialization and view are realizations, not kinds. `view`, `materialized_table`,
`dense_array_asset`, `media_asset`, and `document_asset` MUST NOT be registry kinds.

<a id="ew-resource-instance-model" data-spec-anchor="canonical"></a>
### 2.4 Instance modes

An `instances` object matches exactly one closed form:

| mode | required fields | packet binding |
|---|---|---|
| singleton | `mode: "singleton"`; `presence` is `required` or `optional` | the declaration ID is the only permitted packet `resource_id` |
| catalog | `mode: "catalog"`; `catalog_resource`; `resource_id_column` | zero or more resources bind one-to-one to rows of the named singleton table |
| family | `mode: "family"` | zero or more resources bind only by declaration ID |

A family resource ID is packet-unique and dotted within a namespace the declaration's registry
owns. A catalog resource and column satisfy §2.6.

<a id="ew-resource-presence" data-spec-anchor="canonical"></a>
### 2.5 Presence and dependencies

`core.sessions` is a required singleton. Every other currently incorporated table is an optional
singleton. An extension selects required or optional for each singleton; binding that extension
registry in a packet under [Part 04 §8](../parts/04_packet_catalog.md#ew-packet-registry-bindings)
activates its required declarations. Merely installing a registry does not. A singleton
declaration whose content class is `analysis`, `compatibility_projection` or `documentation` MUST
declare `presence` `optional`.

A packet contains an instance when any of these conditions applies:

- it is a required singleton in an active registry;
- a present declaration names its singleton declaration in `requires`;
- a populated table reference targets its singleton table declaration; or
- a catalog row requires that exact family instance under §2.6.

An absent optional singleton creates no empty table. A present table may contain zero rows unless
another rule requires rows. Catalog and family declarations have no minimum instance count.

Each `requires` member resolves under the same visibility and direction rules as a reference and
names a singleton. A present instance requires the transitive closure of those dependencies.
Dependency cycles are permitted only where every member is a singleton; presence of one then
requires the entire cycle.

<a id="ew-resource-catalog-binding" data-spec-anchor="canonical"></a>
### 2.6 Catalog binding

A catalog declaration's `catalog_resource` MUST name a visible singleton table declaration, and its
`resource_id_column` names one column of that table. That column MUST be a required string
identifier with carried identity and free vocabulary. An extension catalog may
name a core table or one in the same extension; a core catalog MUST NOT name an extension table.
Within one visible core-and-extension context, no two catalog declarations may name the same
table-and-column pair.

For every catalog row, a conforming packet contains exactly one entry whose `resource_id` equals
the row's catalog resource-ID value and whose `declaration_id` names that catalog family. Every
entry in the family matches exactly one row. Catalog resource-ID values and packet resource IDs are
unique.

Four catalog tables carry `resource_id`: `core.array_assets`, `analysis.array_assets`,
`core.media_assets` and `core.sidecars`. In the first three an assigned integer, `array_asset_id`
or `media_asset_id`, remains the primary key and row identity, and its `*_name` the source-label
bridge. In `core.sidecars`, `resource_id` is the primary key and the table has neither. None of the
four carries `uri` or `format`: location belongs to Part 04 and physical encoding to Part 03, and
`core.sidecars.media_type` states no physical encoding that Part 03 defines.

A registry MUST declare every column named `resource_id` as a required `string` identifier with
carried identity and free vocabulary, whether the column is a catalog table's field or references
one. Neither §4's integer `_id` rule nor the integer type §5.3 gives an identifier applies to a
column of that name.

<a id="ew-resource-provenance-policy" data-spec-anchor="canonical"></a>
### 2.7 Lineage policy

The `lineage` field governs the packet entry's `derived_from`. `forbidden` requires absence,
`optional` permits presence, and `required` requires presence. A present lineage is a nonempty array of
distinct existing packet resource IDs.

Lineage edges obey this relation:

| derived class | permitted source classes |
|---|---|
| `core` | `core` |
| `extension` | visible `core` and same-extension `extension` |
| `analysis` | `core`, `extension`, `analysis` |
| `compatibility_projection` | `core`, `extension`, `analysis` |
| `documentation` | none |

The lineage graph contains no self-edge or cycle. Because every analysis resource has nonempty
lineage, every finite analysis chain terminates in canonical core or extension resources. A writer
and validator apply this policy without regard to kind. [Part 04 §10](../parts/04_packet_catalog.md#ew-packet-manifest-conformance) has a reader expose the
resolved facts.

<a id="ew-amendment-groups" data-spec-anchor="canonical"></a>
### 2.8 Amendment groups

Every `table` object carries `amendment_groups`, an array of **group lists**, beside its columns,
primary key, references and rules. A group list is a nonempty array of distinct column names of the
table. Under one group list, the rows whose listed columns hold equal values, a null equal to a
null, form one **group**. A group list **carries** each declared reference whose source columns are
all members of the list, where the referenced table has a column named by each other member: the
referenced row is then the one the group describes. A version **holds** a group where it holds a row
of the group, or where the list carries at least one declared reference to a table that does not
declare `[]` and each such reference resolves, on the group's values, to a row that version holds. A
canonical table a version's manifest does not name holds no rows in that version. A table an
amendment may not add rows to declares `[]`.
[Part 05 §15.2](../parts/05_validation_and_import.md#ew-amendment-scope) lets an amendment add rows to a
table only in groups its superseded version does not hold.

A packet holding a stream therefore holds the stream's group of `core.events` before the stream has
an event, since that table's list carries `stream_id`'s reference to `core.event_streams`. A packet
holding a frame holds a basis's group of `core.electrode_coordinates` only where it holds
coordinates in that basis, since the table's first list carries no reference to
`core.coordinate_frames`, which has no `coordinate_basis` column. The lists of `core.intervals`, the
second of `core.unit_quality_labels`, the first of `core.electrode_coordinates` and the second of
`core.unit_coordinates` carry no reference, since an interval kind, a label source and a coordinate
basis name no row, so a group under those is held only by its rows.

A group list MUST name only columns whose presence is `required` or `nullable`. Two group lists of
one table MUST NOT name the same set of columns. A table whose content class is not canonical MUST
declare `[]`, since an amendment may replace such a resource whole.

<a id="ew-column-declaration" data-spec-anchor="canonical"></a>
## 3. Column declaration

Every column MUST declare a name, a type, a presence, and a role. The role and type determine which
other fields the column requires or permits.

| attribute | required | clause |
|---|---|---|
| `name` | yes | §4 |
| `type` | yes | §5 |
| `presence` | yes | §6 |
| `role` | yes | §8.1 |
| `identity` | for identifiers | §8.2 |
| `finite` | for `float32` and `float64` columns | §5.4 |
| `enum` | for closed vocabularies | §9.3 |
| `resolves`, `discriminates`, `discriminates_via` | as applicable for discriminators | §8.3 |
| `unit`, `unit_from`, `clock_from` | where applicable | §8.4 |
| `authority` | where named nomenclatures cover the column | §9.3 |
| `description` | for an open vocabulary | §9.3 |

A description MUST NOT contradict or override a machine declaration. Concise, non-duplicative
wording remains authoring style rather than a registry-conformance comparison of English text.
The one description compared literally is the standard `metadata` description in §8.6.

<a id="ew-logical-column-naming" data-spec-anchor="canonical"></a>
## 4. Naming

Resource names and column names MUST be lowercase snake case. Registry conformance enforces these
relations in both directions where stated:

- an identifier has an `_id` name, and an `_id` name declares an integer identifier, except that
  a column named `resource_id` is a string identifier with carried identity (§2.6);
- a coordinate has a `_tick` name, and a `_tick` name declares a coordinate;
- a structural index has an `_index` name, and an `_index` name declares a structural index or
  sequence position;
- an `_name` name declares a string; and
- an `_uri` name, or the bare name `uri`, declares a string.

A checker determines discriminator behavior from its declaration and MUST NOT infer it
from a `_kind` suffix. Column order, adjacency, reserved-word preferences, plural-name preferences,
and the vocabulary of same-spelled columns are authoring conventions rather than registry
conformance requirements.

A source-provenance label — `local_electrode_label`, `local_channel_label`, `local_unit_label`, `local_interval_label` — is a `string` carrying whatever base the producing software used, and a consumer MUST NOT use it as an offset into any packet resource.

## 5. Type system

### 5.1 Permitted types

A column's type MUST be one of:

```text
string  int32  int64  float32  float64  bool  timestamp  date  json_string
```

<a id="ew-no-nested-types" data-spec-anchor="canonical"></a>
### 5.2 No nested types

A column's type MUST NOT be `list`, `struct`, or `map`. A registry document MUST declare a repeated value, a fact holding zero or more values for one row, as a child table, and MUST NOT declare it as a delimited string or as JSON. No checker verifies this beyond the type prohibition above. A semi-structured value becomes a `json_string` in an optional column.

This keeps a conforming reader able to satisfy every required column without a JSON parser, and keeps every value within reach of a rule: Part 11 binds no collection type and admits no routine that reads inside one, so a rule can check a repeated value, a reference included, only where each value is its own row.
